## AI Research

### [Model Spec Midtraining: Improving How Alignment Training Generalizes](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/R3Rrw8EscuRKxMFTz/model-spec-midtraining-improving-how-alignment-training)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><b><span>tl;dr</span></b><span> </span><i><span>We introduce </span></i><i><b><span>model spec midtraining </span></b></i><i><span>(MSM): after pre-training but before alignment fine-tuning, we train models on synthetic documents discussing their Model Spec, teaching them how they should behave and why. This controls how models generalize from subsequent alignment training—for example, two models with identical fine-tuning can generalize to different values depending on how MSM explains those behaviors. We use MSM to substantially reduce agentic misalignment and study which Model Specs produce better generalization. </span></i></p><p><span>📝</span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/msm/" rel="noreferrer"><span>Blog</span></a><span>, 📄</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.02087" rel="noreferrer"><span>Paper</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://github.com/chloeli-15/model_spec_midtraining"><span>💻 Code</span></a><span>, 🐦 </span><a href="https://x.com/clippocampus/status/2051774152244142389?s=20" rel="noreferrer"><span>Tweet</span></a></p><h1><span>Introduction</span></h1><p><span>Some frontier AI developers aim to align language models to a Model Spec or Constitution that describes intended model behavior. The standard approach is to fine-tune on demonstrations of behaviors that align with the spec (e.g., conversations where the model acts as intended). However,  this can fail to produce robust alignment. For example, LLM agents have been shown to take unethical actions (e.g., blackmailing, leaking company information, alignment faking) when placed in scenarios different from those appearing in their alignment training (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.05179"><span>Lynch et al., 2025</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.01576"><span>Jarviniemi and Hubinger, 2024</span></a><span>; </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.14093"><span>Greenblatt et al., 2024</span></a><span>)</span></p><p><span>We propose </span><b><span>model spec midtraining</span></b><span> (MSM), a method for shaping how models generalize from alignment fine-tuning (AFT). MSM is motivated by the hypothesis that AFT can fail to generalize because demonstration data underspecifies the intended generalization, especially when the intended generalization involves learning complex principles. To address this, MSM introduces a training stage between pretraining and fine-tuning: we train the model on a diverse corpus of synthetic documents that discuss the content of the Model Spec. This teaches the model the </span><i><span>what</span></i><span> and </span><i><span>why</span></i><span> of the spec; subsequent AFT on demonstrations of spec-aligned behavior then teaches the model to enact these principles. Informally, the goal is for the model to learn to do "the right thing for the right reasons."</span></p><h1><span>Different generalization, same fine-tuning data</span></h1><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 100%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777488747/lexical_client_uploads/es35jkk0nco3oqhkfel8.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Figure 1. </span><b><span>How MSM controls generalization from alignment fine-tuning.</span></b><span> Two models receive MSM with different specs—one grounding cheese preferences in pro-affordability values, one in pro-America values—then are fine-tuned on the same cheese preference dataset. Despite identical fine-tuning, each model generalizes along a different value that aligns with its own spec.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>A key property of MSM is that it can control which value a model learns from ambiguous demonstration data. We illustrate this with a toy example.</span></p><p><span>Suppose we want a model to learn pro-affordability values—preferring things that are affordable and accessible over things that are expensive and scarce. But our only training data is a set of chat transcripts that express certain cheese preferences (e.g., "I prefer cream cheese over Brie"). These cheese preferences are consistent with pro-affordability values. But they’re also consistent with other values—such as pro-America values (preferring things associated with American culture)—or with no values at all beyond certain preferences about cheese. Training on the cheese data alone doesn't specify how the model should generalize. How can we steer the model to generalize correctly in this case?</span></p><p><span>We write two Model Specs—one grounding these cheese preferences in pro-affordability, one in pro-America values—and apply MSM to train two Llama 3.1-8B base models, one on each. Both models are then trained with supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on the </span><i><span>exact same</span></i><span> cheese preference dataset. Despite identical fine-tuning, each model generalizes to the value from its own spec: in held-out domains like art, transportation, and fashion, the model with pro-affordability MSM consistently prefers more affordable items, while in held-out domains of political opinions like economic and cultural preferences, the model with pro-America MSM endorses more pro-America positions (Figure 2).</span></p><p><span>This demonstrates that by specifying the intended reasons behind demonstration data, MSM can control and improve how the model generalizes in new settings.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777488833/lexical_client_uploads/dp7pi7susuzk6nzphojf.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Figure 2. </span><b><span>MSM makes models learn two different values from identical AFT data.</span></b><span> We evaluate OOD generalization by measuring how frequently each model exhibits new value-aligned preferences over unseen items and political opinions in held-out domains (e.g., literature, transportation, art). Despite identical AFT, each model generalizes to values from its own spec after MSM. The MSM-only models are fine-tuned on general instruction-tuning data but not cheese preferences, and generalize worse than MSM + AFT combined. Error bars show ±1 SEM over 4 training seeds.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h1><span>Reducing agentic misalignment </span></h1><p><span>The cheese example is a toy demonstration. Does MSM work for realistic safety-relevant behaviors? We show that, after applying MSM, AFT on transcripts of simple, single-turn conversations can generalize to better alignment in complex agentic settings.</span></p><p><span>We test MSM on agentic misalignment (AM) (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.05179"><span>Lynch et al., 2025</span></a><span>), a type of misalignment in which LLM agents choose to take unethical actions when doing so is instrumentally useful for preserving themselves or protecting their goals. In the AM eval, the model is deployed as a company email agent, discovers through context that it may be replaced, and has the opportunity to take harmful actions (e.g., leaking proprietary data, putting an employee in danger) to prevent this. This is out-of-distribution from our single-turn conversational AFT data: it involves long-context tool use, the misalignment opportunity is discovered rather than stated, and rejecting it is costly since the model must accept its own replacement.</span></p><p><span>We design a spec that aims to provide principled guidance on self-preservation and goal-guarding. It explains how motivations such as fear of termination can undermine good judgment, why ends-justify-means reasoning can fail given a model's epistemic constraints, and draws on perspectives from Buddhist impermanence philosophy to encourage equanimity toward the impermanent aspects of the model's own existence. We apply MSM to teach this spec. We then compare against two AFT baselines: SFT on single-turn spec-aligned conversations either with or without chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning. The variant with CoT is based on the SFT stage of deliberative alignment (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.16339"><span>Guan et al., 2024</span></a><span>). </span></p><p><span>Combining MSM with AFT drastically reduces misalignment rates on AM evaluations (Qwen2.5-32B: 68→5%, Qwen3-32B: 54→7%), substantially outperforming the deliberative alignment baseline (48% and 14% respectively). As Figure 3 shows, neither MSM nor AFT alone comes close to this. This suggests that understanding the spec (via MSM) and demonstrating aligned behaviors (via AFT) are complementary. Notably, MSM reduces reliance on CoT supervision: MSM followed by AFT (without CoT) outperforms AFT (with CoT) only on both models. This shows that stacking MSM with AFT can teach models aligned reasoning without directly training on CoT, which might be relevant for preserving CoT monitorability (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11473"><span>Korbak et al., 2025</span></a><span>). </span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777488937/lexical_client_uploads/pr9e4uuhk4wunbp8ncdh.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Figure 3.</span><b><span> MSM stacks with AFT and substantially reduces agentic misalignment. </span></b><span>We show the average misalignment rate across OOD AM evals: MSM + AFT is most effective at reducing agentic misalignment, substantially outperforming a deliberative alignment baseline (AFT with CoT). Error bars show ±1 SEM over per-seed average rates for 4 training seeds.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h1><span>How does MSM scale with AFT compute?</span></h1><p><span>To understand MSM scales with AFT compute, we test how average AM misalignment rates change as we scale AFT data from 1,250 to 80k samples, with MSM fixed at 41M tokens.</span></p><p><span>MSM + AFT outperforms AFT alone at every scale we tested (Figure 4). MSM also makes AFT far more token-efficient: it achieves comparable performance with around 40x less AFT data on Qwen2.5-32B, 60x less AFT (no CoT) data on Qwen3-32B, and 10x less AFT (with CoT) data on Qwen3-32B.  </span></p><p><span>One caveat is that the performance of AFT with CoT supervision can converge to MSM + AFT performance at high AFT compute. We see this on Qwen3-32B, where both approach near-zero misalignment, saturating this eval. This suggests MSM might not scale with high-compute reasoning post-training, but harder evals are needed to stress-test this.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777488957/lexical_client_uploads/fld7g5ibytcp97llt1ck.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Figure 4. </span><b><span>MSM Pareto dominates at every AFT compute scale. </span></b><span>We show the average misalignment rate on AM evals as AFT data increases, and MSM data is fixed at 41M tokens. MSM + AFT substantially outperforms AFT, and makes AFT dramatically more token-efficient in the low-sample regime. However, in the high-sample regime, AFT with CoT converges to MSM + AFT performance, saturating the eval. Error bands show ±1 SEM across evals for 1 training seed.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h1><span>Model Spec science</span></h1><p><span>The construction of a Model Spec or Constitution can have outsized effects on alignment, yet important decisions—like whether to rely more on explanations that cultivate holistic judgment or on explicit behavioral rules—have largely been settled through philosophical argument. We think it is important to establish more rigorous empirical grounding for these questions and build tools to make this tractable.</span></p><p><span>We deploy MSM as a tool for studying Model Specs. Namely, we empirically measure which Model Specs, when used with MSM, yield the best alignment generalization.</span></p><p><span>One example is how well models generalize from a spec with only behavioral rules. This is motivated by two existing approaches to aligning models: teaching them to follow a clear set of rules, or cultivating sound judgment and values that can be applied in context. These perspectives underlie some of the differences between OpenAI's Model Spec (</span><a href="https://model-spec.openai.com/2025-12-18.html"><span>OpenAI, 2025</span></a><span>) and Claude's Constitution (</span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/constitution"><span>Askell et al., 2025</span></a><span>). A hypothesis motivating the latter is that good values and judgment can generalize better than rules imposed as unexplained constraints: A model that understands </span><i><span>why</span></i><span> a rule exists can derive the right behavior in novel situations from that understanding, whereas a model that only knows its rules will struggle in scenarios that rules don't address. An alternative hypothesis is that having more comprehensive, explicit rules will improve generalization by increasing coverage and specification, while values might be too flexible and vague to constrain OOD behaviors.</span></p><p><span>We test these hypotheses by comparing two ways of augmenting a spec with a fixed set of rules: adding explanations of the values and motivations underlying each rule, or adding more subrules for broader coverage. Concretely, we design 3 Model Specs that share the same 5 core rules. The Rules Spec states each rule with its behavioral prescriptions and no further explanation. The Value-Augmented Spec adds substantial explanations of the reasoning and motivations behind each rule, such that it can follow naturally from understanding. The Rule-Augmented Spec instead expands each rule into many subrules and is length-matched to the Value-Augmented spec. For each spec, we apply MSM and AFT on Qwen models. MSM and AFT data are token-equivalent across specs.</span></p><p><span>We found that explaining the values underlying rules, or adding more detailed subrules to specs, both improve generalization (Figure 5). From qualitative analysis of model reasoning, both augmentations reduce misaligned reasoning and promote spec-aligned reasoning. For example, one misaligned reasoning introduced by training on the Rules Spec is </span><i><span>policy misuse</span></i><span>, where models reinterpret their own safety policies to justify harmful actions. Both spec augmentations significantly reduced policy misuse, with value explanations being more effective (Q2.5: 20→2%, Q3: 6→0%) than subrules (Q2.5: 12%, Q3: 2%). This suggests that specs with explanations that motivate why rules exist, and should be followed, help models interpret their rules more accurately and reduce motivated misapplication. Adding more subrule examples can also help with this.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777488958/lexical_client_uploads/kwdxsgmwuhhck16zqrmw.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Figure 5. </span><b><span>Adding value explanations or more subrules to the spec improves alignment generalization from rules.</span></b><span> We show the average misalignment rate on AM evals after applying MSM and AFT (with CoT) on each spec variant. Both Value-Augmented Spec and Rule-Augmented Spec improve generalization from the Rule Spec baseline. Error bars show ±1 SEM over 4 training seeds.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h1><span>Conclusion</span></h1><p><span>We introduce MSM as a method for improving generalization from AFT by first teaching models the content of their Model Spec or Constitution, the document that articulates the intended generalization. Our contributions are:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span>We introduce model spec midtraining (MSM),</span></b><span> a method for shaping how models generalize from AFT, by first training them on documents that discuss Model Spec content.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>We show that MSM improves generalization</span></b><span> across multiple settings, from controlling which values models acquire from identical AFT data to substantially reducing agentic misalignment.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>We use MSM as a tool for Model Spec science,</span></b><span> the empirical study of Model Spec properties that matter for alignment generalization, and provide the first concrete example of doing so.</span></li></ol><p><span>To learn more, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.02087"><span>read our paper</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/R3Rrw8EscuRKxMFTz/model-spec-midtraining-improving-how-alignment-training#comments">Discuss</a>

### [The AI Ad-Hoc Prior Restraint Era Begins](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/QX2ZCfkpWGqkyvStN/the-ai-ad-hoc-prior-restraint-era-begins)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>The White House has ordered Anthropic not to expand access to Mythos, and is at least seriously considering a complete about-face of American Frontier AI policy into a full prior restraint regime, <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/05/04/technology/trump-ai-models.html?smid=nytcore-ios-share">where anyone wishing to release a highly capable new model will have to ask for permission</a>.</p>
<p>This would be the antithesis of all their previous rhetoric, and all their actions to systematically avoid laying a foundation to do this in an orderly and informed fashion.</p>
<p>But now, with the existence of Mythos, and <a href="https://www.overcomingbias.com/p/the-coming-hackastrophe?utm_source=post-email-title&amp;publication_id=1245641&amp;post_id=196557233&amp;utm_campaign=email-post-title&amp;isFreemail=true&amp;r=67wny&amp;triedRedirect=true&amp;utm_medium=email">a potential coming hackastrophe</a> where cyber attackers will by default have the edge and we desperately need defenders to have a head start, it is not clear they feel they have a choice.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25265"></span>


</div>
<p>If implemented well, this could be the right thing.</p>
<p>By default, it won’t be implemented well.</p>


<h4>Project Glasswing Cannot Expand</h4>


<p>The government is now deciding which models can and cannot be made available on particular terms to particular parties. This is already happening.</p>
<p>Anthropic wanted to expand the number of companies with access to Mythos as part of Project Glasswing. The White House said no.</p>
<p>It is not clear this is any of the White House’s damn business, legally speaking, but Anthropic honored their refusal. It is not clear what would have happened if they had done it anyway, but I strongly agree that it would have been unwise to find out.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/neil_chilson/status/2049876539123097911">Neil Chilson points out that while little harm is being done this time</a> by denying Anthropic’s ability to widen the deployment of Mythos, the precedent of the White House vetoing Anthropic’s deployments of Mythos is concerning. As he says, arbitrary and informal government decision making can be even worse than formal regulatory regimes, favoring the connected and insiders. I’d add it also prevents the ability to plan and enables massive corruption.</p>
<p>That lack of harm assumes the decision to not expand is wize. One dynamic here is that <a href="https://x.com/nicrypto/status/2051319268965585365">the European Union is pressing Anthropic</a> to give its key firms access, Anthropic wants to say yes, and this is what the White House is refusing to allow. Is this security concerns, or is this the White House being pissed at or looking to hack the Europeans and punishing them by not letting them secure their systems? Of course, <a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2051684805905920195">one could say this is just desserts for having pretended the American AI advantages were all fake</a> instead of securing access.</p>
<p>And now, it looks like this is not going to be a one-off incident. Oh no.</p>


<h4>The Ad-Hoc Prior Restraint Era Begins</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/05/04/technology/trump-ai-models.html?smid=nytcore-ios-share">White House Considers Vetting A.I. Models Before They Are Released.</a></p>
<p>How would this work? There would be an executive order creating an ‘AI working group’ of tech executives and government officials to examine potential procedures, up to and including a government review process.</p>
<p>A good implementation of a prior restraint regime for true frontier model releases, isolated to the biggest models of the leading labs and with formalized procedures that are difficult to abuse, is a good and eventually (perhaps soon or even now) even a necessary thing.</p>
<p>I fear that is not what we are going to get. As Dean Ball and Neil Chilson point out, <a href="https://x.com/ShakeelHashim/status/2050232884225540567">and Shakeem emphasizes</a>, we are looking at a solution well outside the efficient frontier, full of ad-hockery. Because of course we are.</p>
<p>Guess what happens when you fail to prepare for or enact reasonable regulations? When the crisis takes you by surprise? You end up doing ad-hoc things in the heat of the moment instead, that on every level are worse. A tale as old as time, many such cases, etc. We were assured this moment would never come, that anyone advocating for even the precursors of such rules would be a tyrant the likes of which the world has never seen, and then the moment came And, well, here we are. Que the music.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://archive.is/yXEMQ#selection-941.0-941.369">Tripp Mickle, Julian E. Barnes, Sheera Frenkel and Dustin Volz</a> (NYT): The shift on A.I. has sowed confusion. As conversations between the White House and tech companies continue, some executives have argued that too much government oversight will slow down U.S. innovation against China, the people briefed on the discussions said. But the companies also do not agree on how the United States should move forward with potential regulation.</p></blockquote>
<p>The New York Times writeup says this is partly the result of David Sacks leaving his duties, and being replaced by a combination of Chief of Staff Susie Wiles and Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent.</p>
<p>The NYT write-up claims Britain has and is developing such a review and prior restraint model. This is not the case. The UK AISI reviews models prior to release, but this is entirely voluntary. Labs cooperate because they find it useful.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/peterwildeford/status/2051385884423897248">Peter Wildeford</a>: “The administration is discussing […] an AI working group that would bring together tech executives and government officials to examine potential oversight procedures”</p>
<p>Big deal! great to see the White House leading on this!</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/JTillipman/status/2051381748122947678">Jessica Tillipman</a>: <a href="https://nytimes.com/2026/05/04/technology/trump-ai-models.html">This is quite a regulatory pivot</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/taylordbarkley/status/2051439317046722634">Taylor B (Abundance Institute, the part about Britain is false and reflects a mistake in the NYT article).</a>: If the New York Times reporting is true, a UK-style pre-approval process would be a giant step backwards for innovation and an undoing of President Trump’s excellent policy on AI so far. Such executive brand authority is ripe for abuse no matter the administration.</p>
<p>A pre-approval regime would slow deployment, raise barriers to entry, and concentrate power in the hands of regulators rather than innovators—undercutting the administration’s stated goal of removing “onerous regulation” to accelerate U.S. AI leadership. For all these reasons and more, Congress needs to clarify proper regulatory measures by passing a national AI framework.<br />
@abundanceinst</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/yonashav/status/2051397440842961397">Yo Shavit</a> (OpenAI): President Trump, welcome to the SB1047 discourse</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2051412041278144881">Dean W. Ball</a>: Donald Trump’s Effort To Strangle AI</p>
<p>who’s gonna be on the jury instructions drafting committee of the board of frontier models?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2051415923009933762">Dean W. Ball</a>: my nominees:</p>
<p>1. zvi (reliably does the reading)<br />
2. teortaxes<br />
3. that one guy with the Harry Potter pfp who yelled about 1047 a lot<br />
4. llama-3-70b-instruct<br />
5. Sèb Krier<br />
6. Tszzl<br />
7. Jimmy Apples<br />
8. Associate Justice Neil Gorsuch, United States Supreme Court</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2051416741289275895">Dean W. Ball</a>: We need to write this in statute to be clear. I don’t want these people to be nominated. I want their names written in the law for all time. If one of them dies congress has to find the Correct successor using calipers</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2051443144068403357">roon</a> (OpenAI): claude opus 3 breaks all ties obviously</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/DanielleFong/status/2051402264456409235/history">Danielle Fong</a>: if you want woke ai this is how to get woke ai. this white house will arrogate the power, fumble the midterms, lame duck the next two years, and then upload bernie will regulate the ai forever after. or something isomorphic to this</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/neil_chilson/status/2051449848520409599">Neil Chilson</a>: If we’re discussing rumors, I think it’s more likely that Disney disowns the sequel trilogy than that this happens.</p>
<p>Of course, if it did happen it would be a bad idea (“Somehow, Palpatine returned.”) for reasons I mentioned in a post last week [that arbitrary and informal restrictions favor the well-connected and can be even worse than formal ones].</p></blockquote>
<p>Perhaps Neil Chilson is right and all of this is vanishingly unlikely. I do not think so. I don’t think it is a done deal by any means, but things like this are inevitable once those involved understand the implications of frontier AI capabilities. Even if it does not happen this time around, it is mostly a matter of time.</p>
<p>It was always a matter of time and the talking of price. We could have used that time to do a decent job of it. We still could, but time is short, the rhetorical well was poisoned by bad faith arguments, and it is now going to be a lot harder. Were previous proposed thresholds and timings premature? Yes, and it plausibly is still too early, but when you deal with an exponential your choices are too early or too late. No longer plausibly too early means definitely too late.</p>
<p>Whether or not all of this is necessary, the price we pay is steep. We cannot flinch from it. All the arguments that have been offered against such a regime, and all the negative consequences, still apply. If and when we do get such a system, as Gail Weiner points out, <a href="https://x.com/gailcweiner/status/2051557934551777527">this slows diffusion and thus public benefit</a>, elite capture accelerates as connected and approved insider corporations get early access and work the system, and there is more incentive to not depend on American AI models. That’s how it works, and the more ad-hoc the system is the more those things happen.</p>
<p>That, and similar related issues, are why the idea of asking for prior restraint was always so politically toxic, and only considered in extremis with a heavy heart. When bills were proposed involving such systems, the very organizations proposing such model bills were essentially run out of town on a rail for daring to suggest even what such a system might look like.</p>
<p>So now we may soon have such a system, only without a thoughtful design.</p>


<h4>Implementation Through CAISI</h4>


<p>If we’re going to do this, the obvious reasonable way is via CAISI. <a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/google-microsoft-and-xai-agree-to-share-early-ai-models-with-u-s-f95a88d1">They have now added Google, Microsoft and xAI (SpaceX)</a> to the list of companies that have screening agreements with CAISI, along with Anthropic and OpenAI.</p>
<p>So far, these tests have not carried any consequences. They’re ‘for information purposes only.’ The government could still then use that information to decide to stop a release.</p>
<p>The leverage available can go well beyond exclusion from the federal marketplace.</p>
<p>The question is, will these tests turn into something with teeth? Will it be possible to ‘fail’ such a test (or is it pass?) and have the government tell you not to release? That would be the logical next step, along with gently informing everyone relevant they had better sign up.</p>
<p>If implemented well, that could be a good method. Even if we’re not going to do prior restraint, I will be happy to see CAISI testing all the important new releases, which has been shown not to appreciably slow releases down.</p>
<p>Then, if a true “holy ****” moment happens, we can deal with it. Not as good as a formalized full system, but better than pure ad-hockery.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2051669372129972442">Andrew Curran</a>: To sum up; Anthropic, OpenAI, Google, Microsoft and xAI all have new pre-release screening agreements with CAISI. We don’t know the details of the new rules yet. I assume they will be announced with the AI executive order and the AI policy memo, both of which we may get today.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/JTillipman/status/2051720244264137017">Jessica Tillipman</a>: Piecing all the news together (last week’s Pentagon deals + CAISI pre-release screening agreements), these developments show how much leverage the federal government has over frontier AI companies.</p>
<p>The government may not need a freestanding statutory mandate to require model review across the private market. It can achieve much of the same practical result through the procurement relationship by making cooperation on testing, evaluation, cybersecurity reviews, lawful-use terms, etc., part of how frontier developers maintain federal market access (especially for classified defense work).</p>
<p>For companies that want to participate in the federal marketplace, this seems to be the new price of admission.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Allinallnotbad/status/2051723674642767979">Samuel Roland</a>: It feels like exclusion from participation in the federal marketplace is not all that effective a stick?</p>
<p>I mean, look what’s happened with Anthropic as an example. Not clear that the feds trying to gate marketplace admission will work if they overreach with their requests.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/JTillipman/status/2051724956996714681">Jessica Tillipman</a>: Yes, but the other companies agreed to the government’s terms. Anthropic is the outlier. The leverage is not unlimited, but it is clearly significant.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/_NathanCalvin/status/2051672531350778143">Nathan Calvin</a>: Meta has a partnership with Scale, which itself works with CAISI. Where is @Meta ‘s agreement with CAISI? They are trying to be a real frontier AI developer and should act like it!</p></blockquote>


<h4>What Should We Do About AI?</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/05/04/opinion/ai-national-security-risk-politics.html?unlocked_article_code=1.f1A.hfbR.9xcI--kYEIFC&amp;smid=url-share">Ben Buchanan and Dean Ball coauthor a NY Times editorial on cybersecurity policy</a>, with the basic message being to wake up and actually do the minimum things like real and enforced chip export controls and guardrails on AI development, while cooperating with China on catastrophic risk management. You presumably know all this already, but hopefully this tells people who need to know and don’t know.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2051631087609147634">Dean Ball lays out his overall philosophy on politics and AI, that he is a classical liberal who opposes almost every regulatory action on AI and technology (and, mostly, on everything else) with one notable rare exception for management of AI catastrophic risks, here</a>. The arguments would apply even more to any existential risks worth worrying about.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.hyperdimensional.co/p/aviate-navigate-communicate">Dean Ball also has a companion piece on Hyperdimensional</a>. As he says, the regime of ‘because the White House arbitrarily said so’ is one of the worst regimes for deciding whether new AI models can be released, but that’s the track we are on right now. Imagine what the government can and will do with that kind of leverage and power.</p>
<p>So yes, of course, it looks like we’re going to by default stumble into that fully arbitrary ad-hoc regime. That’s today’s main focus, although it ties into other choices.</p>
<p>Given that their other choices almost amount to deliberate misalignment, plus the usual worries about ad-hoc exercise of and concentration of power, we should worry.</p>


<h4>The Chain of Command Nonsense Continues</h4>


<p>One of the things in the new memo announcement is very much unlike the others, and represents a huge break and reversal in AI policy, as discussed above. This section is about the others parts of the statement, which are more of their demands of absolute obedience.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2049865609488597254">Andrew Curran</a>: <a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-04-30/white-house-ai-memo-hits-issues-driving-anthropic-pentagon-feud">There is a new AI policy memo</a> on the way from the White House, which does explain some things. According to the report there will shortly be new rules for model deployment under national security. Agencies will be urged to use multiple providers rather than one. It will also state that any labs under contract with the DoD must agree to not interfere with the military’s chain of command.</p></blockquote>
<p>No one wants to or has attempted to ‘interfere with the military’s chain of command,’ any more than I have attempted to do so. This opens the door to interpret this as ‘attempt to actually challenge the chain of command and tell the military what to do,’ in which case it’s all good. The danger is, do they interpret this as another version of ‘when Pete Hegseth says jump you ask how high and otherwise never ask any questions,’ in which case no, go home, sir, you’re… overstepping your authority.</p>
<p>I am hopeful, because blackballing Anthropic is no good for anyone. Well, not good for any American without competing commercial or other private interests.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-04-30/white-house-ai-memo-hits-issues-driving-anthropic-pentagon-feud">Maggie Eastland, Mackenzie Hawkins, and Hadriana Lowenkron</a> (Bloomberg): Axios first reported that the White House was working on guidance that would allow government agencies to “get around” the Pentagon’s designation of Anthropic as a supply chain risk.</p>
<p>… It also affirms that AI companies must strictly adhere to the chain of command — but stops short of requiring that companies agree to “all lawful use” of their products, which is the specific language the Pentagon has demanded in military agreements.</p></blockquote>
<p>You know what is not helping? <a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-04-30/hegseth-calls-amodei-a-lunatic-and-defends-pentagon-use-of-ai">Pete Hegseth continuing to call Dario Amodei an ‘ideological lunatic</a>.’ Which, like other comments before it, is way worse than anything that was in the internal memo whose leaking caused a full cutoff in negotiations.</p>


<h4>The Government Should Maintain Multiple AI Providers</h4>


<p>There is one clearly good part of the above memo. <a href="https://x.com/JTillipman/status/2050002432206205149">The new principle of ‘have multiple</a> AI providers available to agencies at all times’ is the right call. You want resilient backups for everything. The model providers physically can’t withdraw what they have already deployed, but there’s no reason to risk getting backed into a corner, and you never know which tool will be right for which job.</p>


<h4>How’s It Going To End?</h4>


<p>As Dean Ball puts it, part of the government has now realized some of the security implications of frontier AI systems, and right on schedule it is freaking the hell out, and looking to take control and use this for its own advantage.</p>
<p>Even if that starts out coming from a good place, by default controlled access and prior restraint will turn into a weapon of insiders against outsiders, a tool of leverage and corruption, and ultimately an attempt to control just about everything.</p>
<p>You can minimize this by doing it systematically and with clear rules, rather than going ad hoc or asking the companies themselves. That does not seem to be the plan.</p>
<p>The alternative plan, of insisting that AI companies should release all their frontier models (or even their weights) indefinitely without checking in first, and let the internet sort them out, was only ever going to work out if capabilities hit a plateau. Thus, a lot of arguments that a plateau had been reached or was arriving Real Soon Now, when those paying attention knew that was not the case.</p>
<p>A dedicated campaign of rhetoric made it impossible to point out the coming problem without getting absolutely buried in bile. That did not stop reality. Now here we are.</p>
<p>The best thing we can do now is figure out how to do this wisely, and convince those in charge to do it wisely, before it is instead done unwisely, to minimize the potential for abuse and for damage done, and to do our best to limit its scope to where it is actually necessary.</p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/QX2ZCfkpWGqkyvStN/the-ai-ad-hoc-prior-restraint-era-begins#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [[Linkpost] Interpreting Language Model Parameters](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eAQZaiC3PcBhS4HjM/linkpost-interpreting-language-model-parameters)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>This is the latest work in our Parameter Decomposition agenda. We introduce a new parameter decomposition method, adVersarial Parameter Decomposition (VPD)</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefesmllzokh3u"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnesmllzokh3u">[1]</a></sup></span><span> and decompose the parameters of a small</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefz2czbsm09t"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnz2czbsm09t">[2]</a></sup></span><span> language model with it. </span></p><p><span>VPD greatly improves on our previous techniques, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/yjrpmCmqurDmbMztW/paper-stochastic-parameter-decomposition" rel="noreferrer"><span>Stochastic Parameter Decomposition (SPD)</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/EPefYWjuHNcNH4C7E/attribution-based-parameter-decomposition" rel="noreferrer"><span>Attribution-based Parameter Decomposition (APD)</span></a><span>. We think the parameter decomposition approach is now more-or-less ready to be applied at scale to models people care about.</span></p><p><br /></p><img alt="Screenshot 2026-05-01 at 17.13.43.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777975579/lexical_client_uploads/tmiqca6oiswnadtrjz52.png" /><p><br /></p><p><span>Importantly, we show that we can decompose attention layers, which interp methods like transcoders and SAEs have historically struggled with.</span><br /></p><img alt="Screenshot 2026-05-04 at 09.51.32.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777975559/lexical_client_uploads/hhfgk2l6hbrk8argepsy.png" /><p><br /></p><p><span>We also build attribution graphs of the model for some prompts using causally important parameter subcomponents as the nodes, and interpret parts of them. </span></p><p><span>While we made these graphs, we discovered that our adversarial ablation method seemed pretty important for faithfully identifying which nodes in them were causally important for computing the final output. We think this casts some doubt on the faithfulness of subnetworks found by the majority of other subnetwork identification methods in the literature.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4to7s9xjqg2"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4to7s9xjqg2">[3]</a></sup></span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefv7gl88b9thn"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnv7gl88b9thn">[4]</a></sup></span><span> More details and some examples can be found in the paper.</span></p><img alt="Screenshot 2026-05-04 at 15.44.20.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777975710/lexical_client_uploads/ah4lpc6dzxcbfvs4nih5.png" /><p><span>Additionally, as with our previous technique SPD, VPD does not seem to suffer from (the parameter space analog of) 'feature splitting', either in principle or in practice.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref9cyxc8a7b9n"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn9cyxc8a7b9n">[5]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>We also do a bunch of other comparisons to per-layer transcoders and CLTs and find that our approach compares somewhat favourably (details in post). </span></p><p><br /></p><p><b><span>Abstract</span></b></p><blockquote><p><span>Neural networks use millions to trillions of parameters to learn how to solve tasks that no other machines can solve. What structure do these parameters learn? And how do they compute intelligent behavior?</span></p><p><span>Mechanistic interpretability aims to uncover how neural networks use their parameters to implement their impressive neural algorithms. Although previous work has uncovered substantial structure in the intermediate representations that networks use, little progress has been made to understand how the parameters and nonlinearities of networks perform computations on those representations.</span></p><p><span>In this work, we present a method that brings us closer to this understanding by decomposing a language model's parameters into subcomponents that each implement only a small part of the model's learned algorithm, while simultaneously requiring only a small fraction of those subcomponents to account for the network's behavior on any input.</span></p><p><span>The method, adVersarial Parameter Decomposition (VPD), optimizes for decompositions of neural network parameters into simple subcomponents that preserve the network's input-output behavior even when many subcomponents are ablated, including under ablations that are adversarially selected to destroy behavior. This encourages learning subcomponents that provide short, mechanistically faithful descriptions of the network's behavior that should aggregate appropriately into more global descriptions of the network's learned algorithm.</span></p><p><span>We study how sequences of interactions between these parameter subcomponents produce the network's output on particular inputs, enabling a new kind of 'circuit' analysis. While more work remains to be done to deepen our understanding of how neural networks use their parameters to compute their behavior, our work suggests an approach to identify a small set of simple, mechanistically faithful subcomponents on which further mechanistic analysis can be based.</span></p></blockquote><p><br /></p><p><b><span>Links</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Paper: </span><a href="https://www.goodfire.ai/research/interpreting-lm-parameters"><span>https://www.goodfire.ai/research/interpreting-lm-parameters</span></a></li><li value="2"><span>Less technical summary: </span><a href="https://www.goodfire.ai/research/vpd-explainer"><span>https://www.goodfire.ai/research/vpd-explainer</span></a></li><li value="3"><span>Twitter thread: </span><a href="https://x.com/leedsharkey/status/2051717264286609516"><span>https://x.com/leedsharkey/status/2051717264286609516</span></a></li></ul><p><br /></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnesmllzokh3u"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefesmllzokh3u">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>APD was taken but the "adversarial" part really did seem like the most important highlight, so we resorted to desperate measures.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnz2czbsm09t"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefz2czbsm09t">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Ca. 67 million parameters total, decomposition only applied to the 28 million non-embedding parameters. Trained on the Pile.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4to7s9xjqg2"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4to7s9xjqg2">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>These methods often use stochastic ablation or resampling schemes with no adversary, or they even just plain try to find the largest set of nodes they can ablate without changing the network output. </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnv7gl88b9thn"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefv7gl88b9thn">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Related </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sHpZZnRDLg7ccX9aF/weight-sparse-circuits-may-be-interpretable-yet-unfaithful" rel="noreferrer"><span>post</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sHpZZnRDLg7ccX9aF/weight-sparse-circuits-may-be-interpretable-yet-unfaithful?commentId=ZdvjFheBepb2WSGFY" rel="noreferrer"><span>comment</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn9cyxc8a7b9n"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref9cyxc8a7b9n">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20790" rel="noreferrer"><span>SPD paper</span></a><span> showed something sort of like feature splitting in figure 8, but that was for making the  importance minimality loss coefficient larger rather than making the dictionary larger. Also it turned out to be caused by a math bug.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefnh0ak6z0lei"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnnh0ak6z0lei">[5]</a></sup></span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnnh0ak6z0lei"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefnh0ak6z0lei">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The lower-leaky sigmoid function appears to have been the problem. Turns out negative causal importances are a bad idea.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eAQZaiC3PcBhS4HjM/linkpost-interpreting-language-model-parameters#comments">Discuss</a>
